It compares methods like continual pretraining, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning, finding that reinforcement learning outperforms others in promoting cross-lingual consistency and generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the issue of cross-lingual factual inconsistency in large language models, where models fail to express world knowledge reliably in languages other than English.

Method: The methods compared are light continual pretraining (CPT), supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) for improving cross-lingual factual recall.

Result: The result shows that GRPO consistently outperforms SFT in improving cross-lingual consistency and generalization to unseen languages, while CPT on parallel data yields limited additional gains.

Conclusion: The conclusion is that GRPO is effective in promoting more shared cross-lingual representations by reorganizing multilingual routing, reducing language specialization in MLP layers and attention heads.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) trained predominantly on English data encode substantial world knowledge, yet often fail to express it reliably in other languages, a phenomenon known as cross-lingual factual inconsistency. To study and address this, we introduce PolyFact, a large-scale parallel multilingual factual QA dataset containing 100K Wikidata-grounded facts across 12 typologically diverse languages. Using PolyFact, we compare light continual pretraining (CPT), supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) for improving cross-lingual factual recall in Qwen-2.5-7B and OLMo-2-1124-7B. We find that GRPO consistently outperforms SFT, improving both cross-lingual consistency and generalization to unseen languages, while CPT on parallel data yields limited additional gains. Mechanistic analyses further show that GRPO reorganizes multilingual routing by reducing language specialization in MLP layers and attention heads, thereby promoting more shared cross-lingual representations. We release our code, models, and dataset.

</details>


### [92] [Re-Centering Humans in LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06614)
*Lechen Zhang,Jiarui Liu,Tal August*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates the personalization abilities of large language models (LLMs) using both synthetic and human data, revealing limitations in attribute extraction, pairing, and personalized response generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the gap in LLM personalization performance between synthetic and human data, and to improve evaluation methods.

Method: The authors collected 550 human conversations and 19,069 judgments across three stages of personalization, and introduced two lightweight training-based interventions to improve automated evaluation.

Result: The study found that LLMs struggle with attribute extraction, attribute pairing, and generating personalized responses that humans judge as better than generic responses. Learned reward models achieved only modest correlation with human ratings.

Conclusion: The paper highlights the need for human-aligned personalization quality judgments and provides a foundation for studying how models should extract, select, and incorporate user information to improve LLM personalization.

Abstract: Despite growing interest, most evaluations of large language models' (LLMs') personalization abilities have relied on synthetic data. It remains unclear how well current personalization systems work for real users. In this paper, we study the gap in LLM personalization performance when using synthetic versus human data. We collect human conversations (550 conversations) and judgments across three stages of personalization: extracting user attributes from conversations (5,949 judgments), pairing relevant attributes with new prompts (11,919), and incorporating relevant attributes into a personalized response (1,101). Incorporating human data reveals system limitations at each stage. Models struggle to extract attributes from human conversations, disagree with human judgments on relevant attributes, and generate personalized responses that humans judge no better than generic responses (though that LLM judges widely rate as better). We introduce two lightweight training-based interventions that shift automated personalization evaluation closer to human data in our first two stages. However, in our third stage we find that learned reward models achieve only modest correlation with human ratings, suggesting that human-aligned personalization quality judgments are difficult to model directly. Our collected data provides a foundation for studying how models should extract, select, and incorporate user information in ways that humans find useful.

</details>


### [93] [UnpredictaBench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Distributional Randomness in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06622)
*Amirhossein Abaskohi,Amirhossein Dabiriaghdam,Liang Luo,Ellie Dingqiao Wen,Lele Wang,Giuseppe Carenini,Peter West*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces UnpredictaBench, an evaluation framework to test large language models' ability to capture true underlying distributions, highlighting a significant gap in their distributional sampling capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing use of large language models (LLMs) as substitutes for other entities (e.g., humans in economic simulations) requires them to capture the unpredictability of real systems, which is currently not adequately addressed by existing models.

Method: The authors introduce UnpredictaBench, a benchmark consisting of 448 problems, including canonical statistical distributions, distributions induced by stochastic programs, and natural-language scenarios that describe random processes. They also propose KS@N, a general-purpose evaluation metric based on the Kolmogorov-Smirnov statistical test.

Result: The evaluation of open and proprietary models shows a large spread in distributional capabilities, with scores ranging from near 0 to over 20% at KS@100. No model achieved over 40% at KS@100, indicating significant headroom in distributional sampling as a capability.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that even simple distributional simulation remains challenging for LLMs, making UnpredictaBench a necessary first step toward using LLMs as stand-ins for complex systems.

Abstract: We introduce UnpredictaBench, an evaluation that tests the ability of large language models (LLMs) to capture true underlying distributions. As LLMs are increasingly used as substitutes for other entities (e.g., for humans in economic simulations), the tendency of many models to collapse towards a single plausible answer means a failure to capture the unpredictability of real systems. Recent work on improving output diversity is insufficient for this setting: simulation requires samples that are calibrated to a target distribution, not merely varied outputs. UnpredictaBench isolates a simplified but fundamental version of this problem: sampling outcomes from individual target distributions, including canonical statistical distributions, distributions induced by stochastic programs, and natural-language scenarios that describe random processes. We introduce 448 such problems together with KS@N, a general-purpose evaluation metric that quantifies how well a model outputs approximate black-box target distributions via the Kolmogorov-Smirnov statistical test. This is the rate at which we fail to reject model samples of size N against ground-truth samples, with larger N indicating greater difficulty. Tested across open and proprietary models, we find a large spread in distributional capabilities. For instance, when models generate samples of size 100 (KS@100, our standard metric), scores range from near 0 to over 20%. No model is able to achieve over 40% at KS@100, showing significant headroom in distributional sampling as a capability. Although adding reasoning can somewhat increase scores, we find no immediate solution for this issue. UnpredictaBench shows that even simple distributional simulation remains challenging, making it a necessary first step toward using LLMs as stand-ins for complex systems.

</details>


### [94] [How Language Models Fail: Token-Level Signatures of Committed and Persistent Reasoning Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06635)
*Tanvi Thoria,Kiana Jafari,Marc R. Schlichting,Mykel J. Kochenderfer*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes failures in language model reasoning, identifying two types of failures: committed failure and persistent uncertainty, and proposes a framework for detecting these failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and characterize failures in language model reasoning, and to develop a framework for detecting these failures.

Method: The authors use token-level uncertainty signals to analyze failures in language model reasoning, and identify two empirically distinguishable processes: committed failure and persistent uncertainty.

Result: The authors found that failures in language model reasoning can be characterized using token-level uncertainty signals, and that their framework's predictions hold in 20 of 23 cases across different model-dataset configurations.

Conclusion: The paper provides a foundation for understanding when LLM reasoning failures become detectable and for adapting detection strategies accordingly, with implications for self-consistency and failure detection.

Abstract: Failures in language model reasoning emerge through distinct processes that leave identifiable signatures in the reasoning trace. We characterize these failures using token-level uncertainty signals, finding they arise through two empirically distinguishable processes. The first is committed failure, in which a model locks onto an incorrect reasoning path early in its trace. A central diagnostic signature is the commitment point, beyond which considering additional tokens hurt rather than help failure detection. In the second, persistent uncertainty, uncertainty instead accumulates throughout, and the full trace is needed to best distinguish failing from successful completions. These signatures reproduce across 23 model-dataset configurations, with the framework's falsifiable predictions holding in 20 of 23 cases, well above chance across both failure modes. Finally, we demonstrate our failure mode framework has direct implications for self-consistency, identifying when uncertainty signals complement it and when it can be selectively skipped. These results offer a foundation for understanding when LLM reasoning failures become detectable and for adapting detection strategies accordingly.

</details>


### [95] [CAF-Gen: A Multi-Agent System for Enriching Argumentation Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06646)
*Jakub Bąba,Jarosław Chudziak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new multi-agent framework, CAF-Gen, is introduced to improve Argument Mining by generating richer argument structures compliant with the Carneades Argumentation Framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Argument Mining techniques struggle to capture complex structural information required by advanced argumentation frameworks.

Method: An automated multi-agent framework, CAF-Gen, using an iterative Creator-Reviewer pipeline to enrich shallow argument structures.

Result: The framework achieves strong alignment with original annotations and produces structurally richer models.

Conclusion: The multi-agent system provides a robust methodology for automated modeling of formal argumentation, overcoming limitations of single-pass generation.

Abstract: Formalizing complex reasoning from natural text is one of the central challenges in computational linguistics. It requires systems to understand not just keywords but also the context and complex reasoning embedded in a text. Current Argument Mining (AM) techniques identify basic claims and premises, yet they often struggle to capture the richer structural information required by advanced schemas such as the Carneades Argumentation Framework (CAF), which incorporates features such as premise types, proof standards, and argument schemes. We address this limitation by introducing CAF-Gen, an automated multi-agent framework designed to enrich shallow argument structures into CAF-compliant argument models. By employing an iterative Creator-Reviewer pipeline, a creator agent's output is validated by a critical agent to ensure structural integrity. This multi-agent collaboration is crucial for mitigating the structural instability typical of single-pass generative models. Our experiments demonstrate that the iterative feedback loop improves the quality of the resulting data and achieves strong alignment with the original annotations, while producing structurally richer models. Our findings show that the multi-agent system can overcome the limitations of single-pass generation, providing a robust methodology for the automated modeling of formal argumentation.

</details>


### [96] [The Piggyback Hypothesis of Generalization: Explaining and Mitigating Emergent Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06667)
*Jiachen Zhao,Zhengxuan Wu,Aryaman Arora,Yiyou Sun,David Bau,Weiyan Shi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Piggyback Hypothesis to explain how LLMs over-generalize and introduces Token-Regularized Finetuning (TReFT) to mitigate emergent misalignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The mechanisms behind LLMs' broad over-generalization beyond training examples are unclear, and emergent misalignment (EM) is a pressing issue that needs to be addressed.

Method: The authors propose the Piggyback Hypothesis and validate it through experiments, then introduce TReFT, which regularizes specific token representations during training to mitigate EM.

Result: TReFT reduces EM while preserving in-domain learning across different models and datasets, achieving 33.5% more EM reduction than data interleaving on Llama-3.1-8B.

Conclusion: The paper highlights that LLMs may learn and generalize in unintended ways and suggests a path toward more constrained finetuning, while also calling for further study of how shared input features can piggyback model behavior across domains.

Abstract: The mechanisms behind LLMs' broad over-generalization beyond training examples remain unclear. Emergent misalignment (EM) offers a striking case study: finetuning on narrow tasks induces broad misalignment to semantically-unrelated test domains. In this work, we propose the Piggyback Hypothesis: the chat-template tokens can piggyback the finetuned behaviour onto out-of-domain queries. We validate this hypothesis by showing that subtle perturbations to the prefix (tokens preceding all user queries), or patching the prefix representations with those from the unfinetuned model, can restore alignment without changing the user query. Building on this finding, we propose Token-Regularized Finetuning (TReFT), which regularizes specific token representations during training to mitigate EM. Across different models and multiple EM-inducing datasets, TReFT reduces EM while preserving in-domain learning. On Llama-3.1-8B finetuned on the legal domain, TReFT achieves 33.5% more EM reduction than data interleaving with a retain set of aligned examples. We further show that TReFT extends to other narrow-finetuning settings, including abstention, tool use, and refusal (off-topic generalization is reduced by 54.3% on average), supporting the Piggyback Hypothesis. Broadly, our work highlights that LLMs may learn and generalize in unintended ways and suggests a path toward more constrained finetuning. It also calls for further study of how shared input features can piggyback model behavior across domains.

</details>


### [97] [What Do People Actually Want From AI? Mapping Preference Plurality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06674)
*Julia Sepúlveda Coelho,Scott A. Hale*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes 1,500 open-ended responses from a global dataset to understand what people want from AI systems, revealing that current fine-tuning methods for Large Language Models (LLMs) have significant limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to investigate the limitations of Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) in aligning LLMs with human preferences and values, which aggregates conflicting preferences, relies on unrepresentative samples, and uses binary comparisons.

Method: The authors analyze 1,500 open-ended responses from the PRISM dataset across 75 countries to examine what people actually want from AI systems.

Result: The study finds that people have diverse and often conflicting preferences, with most values requested by fewer than a quarter of respondents, and truthfulness being the sole exception at 49%. The same words have divergent meanings, and certain capabilities and features are controversial.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that current alignment practices have fundamental problems, and the use of universal preference models may be a form of epistemic violence, highlighting the need for more nuanced and contextual approaches to understanding human preferences.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are often fine-tuned through Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) to align with people's preferences and values. However, this method has known limitations: it aggregates conflicting preferences, often relies on unrepresentative samples, and uses only binary comparisons. Analysing 1,500 open-ended responses from the PRISM dataset across 75 countries, we examine what people actually want from AI systems and reveal concrete failures of current methods.
  We find that different people want different things: most values are requested by fewer than a quarter of respondents, with truthfulness the sole exception at 49%. Furthermore, the same words hide divergent meanings: when people describe what they mean by "truthfulness", they reveal distinct, potentially incompatible, epistemological bases, as some ask for sourced claims, some for expert opinions, and some even ask for unpopular views. Certain capabilities, namely how human-like a model behaves, and some features, like AI guardrails, are outright controversial, with some desiring them and others rejecting them. We additionally find that people often use contextual distinctions (what AI should do "by default" versus "if requested") that binary comparisons cannot capture.
  These findings expose fundamental problems in current alignment practices. When 49% request truthfulness but define it differently, this is unlikely to be captured by a single reward model. The persistence of high hallucination rates in well-funded models, despite users' clear demands for accuracy, suggests that current methods fail to identify actual preferences. This paper sheds light on the situated, contested, imperfect signals that are currently being flattened into universal preference models, a practice others have characterised as epistemic violence.

</details>


### [98] [HKJudge: A Legal Discourse-Annotated Corpus for Interpreting What Courts Find, How They Reason, and What They Rule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06679)
*Xi Xuan,Wenxin Zhang,Yufei Zhou,King-kui Sin,Chunyu Kit*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduced HKJudge, a sentence-level expert-annotated legal discourse corpus of Hong Kong judgments, and benchmarked LLMs on two tasks: rhetorical role classification and legal element extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Limited attention on discourse analysis of Hong Kong judgments due to lack of expert-annotated corpora.

Method: Designed a two-tier discourse schema, annotated sentences with rhetorical roles and sentencing elements, and evaluated BERT-based models and LLMs on two tasks.

Result: Demonstrated the value of sentence-level discourse annotation for modeling HK judgments and provided a benchmark evaluation of LLMs.

Conclusion: HKJudge dataset and code are available, providing a rich data foundation for future work on legal judgment prediction.

Abstract: Court judgments are central to legal practice and jurisprudence, yet discourse analysis of Hong Kong judgments has received limited attention, owing largely to the absence of expert-annotated corpora. We introduce the Hong Kong Judgment Discourse Dataset (HKJudge), the first sentence-level expert-annotated legal discourse corpus. HKJudge includes criminal judgments across all five levels of HK's court hierarchy, comprising $\sim$290k sentences and $\sim$6.5 million tokens, fully annotated by legal linguistics experts. We design a two-tier discourse schema that captures what facts a court finds, how it reasons, and what it rules. At the sentence level, each sentence is assigned one of 26 rhetorical roles. At the span level, sentences are further annotated with three sentencing elements (charge, imprisonment term, fine). Ten legal linguistics annotators produced the annotations with an inter-annotator agreement of $κ= 0.8$. We formulate two tasks on HKJudge, termed rhetorical role classification and legal element extraction, and provide the first benchmark evaluation of four BERT-based models, two open-source LLMs under zero-shot and fine-tuning settings, and four commercial LLMs on both tasks. Our work demonstrates the value of sentence-level discourse annotation for modeling the structure of HK judgments and provides a rich data foundation for future work on legal judgment prediction. The HKJudge dataset and code are available at https://github.com/xuanxixi/HKJudge.

</details>


### [99] [Signal-Driven Observation for Long-Horizon Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06708)
*Shubham Gaur,Ian Lane*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Signal-Driven Observation (SDO), a method to reduce context degradation in web agents by compressing observations and only retrieving task-relevant elements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The current architecture of web agents causes progressive context degradation due to the frequent ingestion of large amounts of data, leading to erosion of reasoning capabilities.

Method: The authors propose Signal-Driven Observation (SDO), a dedicated sub-call that reads the full DOM but returns only task-relevant elements and their selectors, and is re-invoked only when a lightweight signal detector fires.

Result: The paper outlines the open problems introduced by SDO and calls on the community to treat observation compression as a core architectural decision in web agent design, but does not provide specific experimental results.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that the current coupling of observation frequency to action frequency is an architectural mistake and that SDO offers a potential solution to mitigate context degradation in web agents.

Abstract: Web agents operating over long horizons ingest raw DOM and accessibility trees -- routinely tens of thousands of tokens -- at every action step, causing progressive context degradation that erodes reasoning well before tasks complete. We argue that this coupling of observation frequency to action frequency is an architectural mistake. Drawing on the insight from Recursive Language Models that querying a document outperforms reading it wholesale, we propose Signal-Driven Observation (SDO): a dedicated sub-call reads the full DOM but returns only task-relevant elements and their selectors, and is re-invoked only when a lightweight signal detector fires -- triggered by URL transitions, newly visible interactive elements, action failures, or exogenous browser events. We outline the open problems SDO introduces and call on the community to treat observation compression as a core architectural decision in web agent design.

</details>


### [100] [Data-Efficient Autoregressive-to-Diffusion Language Models via On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06712)
*Xingyu Su,Jacob Helwig,Shubham Parashar,Atharv Chagi,Lakshmi Jotsna,Degui Zhi,James Caverlee,Dileep Kalathil,Shuiwang Ji*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces an On-Policy Diffusion Language Model (OPDLM) that transforms autoregressive models into diffusion language models using On-Policy Distillation, achieving strong performance with significantly fewer training tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the challenges of transforming autoregressive models (ARLMs) into diffusion language models (DLMs), specifically the distribution shifts and train-inference mismatch, while retaining knowledge acquired by the ARLM during training.

Method: The method introduced is On-Policy Distillation (OPD) for ARLM-to-DLM transformation, where the student model (ARLM with bidirectional attention) generates its own trajectories and the teacher model (original frozen ARLM) distills its knowledge by providing target logits on these trajectories.

Result: The empirical results show that OPDLM requires 15x to 7,000x fewer training tokens with strong performance across a wide variety of tasks, positioning DLM transformation as a form of ARLM post-training.

Conclusion: The conclusion is that OPDLM effectively addresses the challenges of transforming ARLMs into DLMs, eliminating the train-inference mismatch and enhancing knowledge retention from the ARLM, with significant improvements in efficiency and performance.

Abstract: We study the transformation of autoregressive models (ARLMs) into diffusion language models (DLMs). Rather than pretraining from scratch, prior work replaces the causal attention in ARLMs with bidirectional attention and then trains the resulting model using a DLM objective. However, these approaches incur two distribution shifts. First, transitioning from a next-token prediction objective to a DLM objective can discard knowledge acquired by the ARLM during training. Second, standard DLMs suffer from a train-inference mismatch, as the training loss is defined on randomly masked sequences rather than the trajectories encountered at inference produced by confidence-based decoding. To address both challenges, we introduce an On-Policy Diffusion Language Model (OPDLM) in which On-Policy Distillation (OPD) is employed for ARLM-to-DLM transformation. Specifically, OPDLM is trained via self-OPD, where the student, an ARLM with bidirectional attention, generates its own trajectories, and the teacher, the original frozen ARLM, distills its knowledge by providing target logits on these trajectories. By training directly in an on-policy manner, OPDLM eliminates the train-inference mismatch in DLMs, while distillation from the original model enhances knowledge retention from the ARLM. Empirical results demonstrate that OPDLM requires 15x to 7,000x fewer training tokens with strong performance across a wide variety of tasks. OPDLM avoids the prohibitive cost of DLM pretraining and positions DLM transformation as a form of ARLM post-training.

</details>


### [101] [Does Topic Sentiment Cause Perceived Ideology? Comparing Human and LLM Annotations in Political News Articles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06715)
*Upasana Chatterjee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The study examines the causal effect of topic sentiment on perceived political ideology, comparing annotations from human experts, GPT-4o-mini, and Llama-3.3-70B, finding that fine-tuned GPT-4o-mini produces significant treatment effects, but may rely on spurious sentiment-ideology coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether topic sentiment has a causal effect on perceived political ideology and whether this effect depends on who assigns the ideology label.

Method: Using Double Machine Learning (DML) and community-level mediation analysis across four annotation paradigms: expert human annotators, GPT-4o-mini (baseline and fine-tuned), and Llama-3.3-70B.

Result: Fine-tuned GPT-4o-mini achieves the highest classification accuracy (F1=72.48) and produces significant community-level treatment effects and natural direct effects (NDEs) in mediation, while human annotations yield no significant causal effects.

Conclusion: The findings suggest that fine-tuning on ideology-labeled data causes the model to internalize a spurious sentiment-ideology coupling not operative in human judgment, which has implications for the use of LLM annotations as silver labels and proxies for human judgment in causal analyses.

Abstract: We ask whether topic sentiment has a causal effect on perceived political ideology, and whether the answer depends on who assigns the ideology label. Using articles from AllSides, paired with shared sentiment annotations from Llama-3.3-70b-versatile, we compare ideology labels from expert human annotators, GPT-4o-mini (baseline and finetuned), and Llama-3.3-70B. We apply Double Machine Learning (DML) and community-level mediation analysis across all four annotation paradigms. Human annotations yield no significant causal effects at the community level. Fine-tuned GPT-4o-mini achieves the highest classification accuracy (F1=72.48) and is the only annotator paradigm that produces significant community-level treatment effects and significant natural direct effects (NDEs) in mediation. We interpret this as evidence of shortcut learning: fine-tuning on ideology-labeled data causes the model to internalise a spurious sentiment--ideology coupling not operative in human judgment for this task. This coupling is structurally invisible to F1-based evaluation, with implications for the use of LLM annotations as silver labels and as proxies for human judgment in downstream causal analyses.

</details>


### [102] [Modular Monolingual Adaptation using Pretrained Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06738)
*Nalin Kumar,Ondřej Dušek*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A modular approach to adapting monolingual language models to low-resource languages is proposed, which replaces tokens, freezes embeddings, and tunes the rest of the model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to improve the adaptability of pretrained language models to low-resource languages without requiring full model tuning.

Method: The proposed method involves replacing tokens, freezing the corresponding embeddings, and tuning the rest of the model, rather than fine-tuning the whole model.

Result: The proposed approach shows improved performance on natural language understanding tasks (mask filling, NER, and POS) for Scottish Gaelic, Irish, and Quechua, especially for very low-resource languages like Quechua.

Conclusion: The study concludes that the proposed modular approach can be an effective way to adapt pretrained language models to low-resource languages, providing a more efficient and adaptable solution.

Abstract: Building monolingual language models (LMs) for low-resource languages typically relies on adapting pretrained language models (PLMs) by finetuning the whole model on the target language. This approach is widely favored over training from scratch, as it enables effective knowledge transfer. Additionally, prior work has shown that using a language-specific tokenizer can enhance the adaptability. In this work, we hypothesize that full model tuning is often unnecessary and propose a more modular approach. Specifically, we replace the tokens, freeze the corresponding embeddings, and tune the rest of the model. We use Scottish Gaelic, Irish, and Quechua for our experiments, with Quechua being a very low-resource language (8.5k training instances). Evaluation on natural language understanding (NLU) tasks -- mask filling, NER, and POS -- shows that our proposed approach improves performance when adapting models to low-resource languages. Additionally, we provide a comprehensive analysis of the effectiveness of training strategies, the choice of pretrained embeddings, and models.

</details>


### [103] [When to Think Deeply: Inhibitory Deliberation for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06745)
*Zhixuan He,Yue Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new framework, IDPR, is proposed to improve problem-solving performance of large language models by selectively invoking slow reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning large language models can improve problem-solving performance through deliberative inference, but slow reasoning for every input is computationally expensive and often unnecessary.

Method: IDPR generates a concise intuitive answer and uses an inhibition controller to decide whether to release or suppress the answer in favor of slow reasoning, conditioning on the fast answer and fast-side evidence.

Result: IDPR achieves 48.92% accuracy on a mathematical reasoning test set, improving from 47.90%, while invoking slow reasoning on only 8.20% of examples.

Conclusion: IDPR effectively improves problem-solving performance while reducing computational cost, outperforming baselines in accuracy and corrective precision.

Abstract: Reasoning Large Language Models can improve problem-solving performance through deliberative inference, but invoking slow reasoning for every input is computationally expensive and often unnecessary. We propose IDPR, a framework for response-conditioned inhibitory deliberation. IDPR first generates a concise intuitive answer and then uses an inhibition controller to decide whether that specific response should be released or suppressed in favor of slow reasoning. Unlike input-only routers, the inhibition controller conditions on the fast answer and fast-side evidence, including confidence, logit margin, parseability, and generation cost. We train the controller from paired fast-slow outcomes and select the inhibition threshold on a held-out validation set under an accuracy-first slow-call budget. On a held-out 5,000-example mathematical reasoning test set, IDPR invokes slow reasoning on only 8.20% of examples and improves accuracy from 47.90% to 48.92%. Under the same slow-call budget, random routing decreases accuracy to 46.76%, while the strongest confidence-based baseline reaches 48.22%. IDPR also achieves the highest corrective precision, showing that response-conditioned inhibition better identifies fast answers that benefit from slow reasoning.

</details>


### [104] [Evidence Graph Consistency in Retrieval-Augmented Generation: A Model-Dependent Analysis of Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06748)
*Jianru Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new framework called Evidence Graph Consistency (EGC) is proposed to detect hallucinations in large language models by analyzing structural relationships among evidence pieces and answer claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing detection methods for hallucinations in large language models are limited as they rely on flat similarity between generated answers and retrieved passages, ignoring structural relationships.

Method: The EGC framework constructs a local evidence graph per response and computes five structural consistency measures as hallucination indicators.

Result: EGC reveals a consistent model-family split in hallucination patterns across six LLMs, suggesting qualitatively different hallucination patterns and indicating that embedding-based graph consistency cannot serve as a model-independent hallucination detection signal.

Conclusion: The study concludes that EGC is a useful framework for detecting hallucinations, but its effectiveness is model-dependent, and different models exhibit different hallucination patterns.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) reduces but does not eliminate hallucination in large language models. Existing detection methods rely on flat similarity between generated answers and retrieved passages, ignoring structural relationships among evidence pieces and answer claims. We propose Evidence Graph Consistency (EGC), a framework that constructs a local evidence graph per response and computes five structural consistency measures as hallucination indicators. Evaluated on the full question answering split of RAGTruth across six LLMs (5,767 responses), EGC reveals a consistent model-family split: graph consistency features show the expected diagnostic direction for hallucinations in Llama-2 models but exhibit systematic reversal in GPT-4, GPT-3.5, and Mistral-7B. This reversal suggests qualitatively different hallucination patterns across model families and indicates that embedding-based graph consistency cannot serve as a model-independent hallucination detection signal.

</details>


### [105] [PromptPrint: Behavioral Biometrics Through Natural Language Prompting in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06755)
*Shaiv Patel,Kartik Narayan,Vishal Patel*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The authors investigate whether brief prompts to large language models contain a unique signal that can identify the user, finding that lexical representations can identify users with strong performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The traditional focus of authorship attribution research on long-form texts does not apply to interactions with large language models, which are typically brief and task-driven, raising questions about the existence of a stable, author-identifiable signal in such prompts.

Method: The authors introduce PromptPrint, a systematic study using 20,680 real prompts from 1,034 users to analyze prompt-based identity through lexical representations, semantic encoders, and stylometric features.

Result: The study establishes three key findings: lexical representations significantly outperform semantic encoders; stylometric features show a uniqueness-consistency paradox; and identity signals are robust to minor perturbations but degrade under semantic paraphrasing.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate strong identification performance at scale, establishing prompt-based identity as a viable behavioral biometric with important implications for security and privacy in user modeling for LLM interactions.

Abstract: Authorship attribution research has traditionally focused on long-form, expressive texts; however, interactions with large language models (LLMs) are typically brief and task-driven prompts. This raises a fundamental question: do such prompts contain a stable, author-identifiable, and distinctive signal? We introduce PromptPrint, a systematic study of prompt-based identity, the hypothesis that a user's habitual vocabulary, syntax, and discourse patterns form a learnable behavioral biometric. Using 20,680 real prompts from 1,034 users, we establish three key findings. First, lexical representations significantly outperform semantic encoders, supporting the "lexical stability hypothesis": identity is primarily encoded in surface-level word choice rather than abstract intent. Second, stylometric features exhibit a "uniqueness-consistency paradox": users are highly distinctive across the population, yet behaviorally inconsistent across contexts. Third, adversarial analysis reveals a clear vulnerability spectrum: identity signals are robust to minor lexical perturbations but degrade substantially under semantic paraphrasing. Overall, our results demonstrate strong identification performance at scale, establishing prompt-based identity as a viable behavioral biometric. This work introduces a new perspective on user modeling in LLM interactions, with important implications for security and privacy. Data and code will be released upon the acceptance of our work.

</details>


### [106] [A Four-Condition Diagnostic Protocol for Evidence Utilization in Long-Context and Retrieval-Augmented Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06758)
*Haizhou Xia*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a protocol to evaluate how well language models use given evidence to answer questions, finding task-dependent bottlenecks in evidence utilization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current metrics do not adequately assess whether a language model effectively uses provided evidence, as models can answer from memory, fail to use given passages, or cite evidence without properly converting it into an answer.

Method: The paper introduces a four-condition evidence-availability protocol (no evidence, full context, retrieved evidence, and oracle-evidence reference) and uses ONCU as an estimator of evidence advantage, evaluating five models across several datasets.

Result: The study finds that controlled synthetic settings reveal full-context utilization failures, while realistic multi-hop settings expose retrieval-chain coverage failures, with task-dependent bottlenecks in evidence utilization.

Conclusion: The contribution is a diagnostic protocol for evaluating evidence utilization in language models, rather than a single-score leaderboard, helping to identify specific failures in using evidence.

Abstract: Final-answer accuracy, retrieval recall, and citation overlap do not by themselves identify whether a long-context or retrieval-augmented language model used the evidence it was given. A model can answer from parametric memory, fail despite receiving the right passages, or cite evidence without converting it into the requested answer. This paper proposes a matched four-condition evidence-availability protocol--no evidence, full context, retrieved evidence, and oracle-evidence reference--for diagnosing evidence utilization under fixed examples, prompts, score fields, retrieval settings, and validity checks. ONCU is used as a protocol-bound estimator of recovered oracle-reference evidence advantage and is computed only for denominator-valid groups; denominator-free answer, evidence, retrieval, and failure-audit metrics are reported separately. The empirical study evaluates five local open-weight models from the Qwen, Gemma, Llama, and Mistral families across Controlled-ONCU-safe16K, HotpotQA-ONCU, and 2WikiMultiHopQA-ONCU, with 18,000 ONCU-compatible predictions. The main finding is a task-dependent bottleneck split: controlled synthetic settings primarily expose full-context utilization failures, whereas the tested realistic multi-hop settings primarily expose retrieval-chain coverage failures in denominator-free answer and evidence metrics, with ONCU supporting the same direction on oracle-improving groups. The contribution is a diagnostic protocol for separating no-evidence answerability, oracle-evidence recoverability, full-context utilization, and retrieval-conditioned utilization, rather than a single-score leaderboard for long-context or retrieval-augmented systems.

</details>


### [107] [When Better Codebooks Are Not Enough: Predictive Performance and Behavioral Reliability in LLM Political Event Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06781)
*Zixian He,Bharath Raahul Murugesan,Patrick Brandt,Yibo Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The study examines the faithfulness of Large Language Models (LLMs) in coding political events, finding that while clearer codebooks improve performance, accuracy does not guarantee behavioral reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many social-science studies rely on expert-written codebooks to turn text into structured data, but high accuracy does not necessarily make an LLM a faithful coder.

Method: The study tests the effectiveness of expert codebooks operationalized into LLM-friendly forms with clearer definitions, examples, retrieved context, and rules for difficult cases, and evaluates behavioral reliability under controlled changes.

Result: Clearer codebooks substantially improve classification performance, especially for fine-grained event classification, but predictive gains do not fully translate into behavioral reliability.

Conclusion: Codebook-guided LLM systems should be evaluated not only by accuracy, but also by whether they preserve the coding logic that makes coded outputs meaningful for social-science research.

Abstract: High accuracy does not necessarily make an LLM a faithful coder. This issue matters because many social-science studies rely on expert-written codebooks to turn text into structured data. We study this problem in political event coding, a challenging source-target relation classification task beyond ordinary sentence-level classification, where models must determine what one actor did to another using detailed coding rules.
  We test whether expert codebooks become more effective when operationalized into LLM-friendly forms with clearer definitions, examples, retrieved context, and rules for difficult cases. We then evaluate behavioral reliability under controlled changes to label names, codebook order, and label-definition mappings. Clearer codebooks substantially improve classification performance, especially for fine-grained event classification. However, these predictive gains do not fully translate into behavioral reliability. Models may produce valid labels and recover definitions while still failing behavioral reliability tests under controlled codebook changes.
  These findings suggest that codebook-guided LLM systems should be evaluated not only by accuracy, but also by whether they preserve the coding logic that makes coded outputs meaningful for social-science research.

</details>


### [108] [Explain Like I'm 5 or Whatever I Choose: Evaluating the Interactive Potential of Language Model Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06788)
*Indu Panigrahi,Tal August*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new evaluation framework for large language models (LLMs) in scientific information seeking tasks, focusing on generating multiple responses with varying language complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of LLMs assume a single, static chat interface, but as models are integrated into new interfaces, evaluations must incorporate interface-specific criteria.

Method: The authors conducted a formative study with 16 participants and evaluated GPT-5.1, GPT-5 mini, Claude Sonnet 4.5 + Thinking, and DeepSeek-V3.1 by generating 5 responses at different levels of language complexity for 98 scientific queries.

Result: The models vary complexity across responses, but most changes remain inconsistent, with the best performing model (Claude Sonnet 4.5) only shifting reliable complexity measures in the correct direction 46% of the time.

Conclusion: The findings highlight the need for a new evaluation framework that incorporates interface-specific criteria and can assess LLMs' ability to generate responses with varying language complexity.

Abstract: Evaluations of large language models (LLMs) in scientific information seeking tasks have become increasingly use-centric, such as conducting live or multi-turn evaluations with real users. These evaluations still assume a single, static chat interface, but as models are integrated into new interfaces, evaluations must shift to incorporate interface-specific criteria. We propose a new evaluation framework based on a formative study with $16$ participants that tests models' ability to generate multiple responses to one query that differ along an interpretable axis of language (language complexity), inspired by direct manipulation interfaces from human-centered design literature. We evaluate GPT-5.1, GPT-5 mini, Claude Sonnet 4.5 + Thinking, and DeepSeek-V3.1 by generating 5 responses at different levels of language complexity for $98$ scientific queries. While models vary complexity across responses, most changes remain inconsistent, with the best performing model (Claude Sonnet 4.5) only shifting reliable complexity measures in the correct direction $46\%$ of the time. Our findings hold with increased sample size and alternative complexity levels.

</details>


### [109] [TA-RAG: Tone-Aware Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Peer-Support Health Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06794)
*Yong-Bin Kang,Anthony McCosker*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents TA-RAG, a framework that enhances retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with tone awareness for sensitive health communication, making LLM outputs more accessible, empathetic, and tailored.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Factual grounding in RAG is insufficient for sensitive peer-support health communication; responses must also be accessible, stigma-free, empathetic, and tailored.

Method: The TA-RAG framework embeds explicit tone control into a RAG pipeline through four components: stigma-free rewriting, readability adjustment, recipient adaptation, and empathy rephrasing, without requiring model fine-tuning.

Result: TA-RAG's components improve targeted communication quality while preserving key content, suggesting prompt-based tone control as a potential direction for making RAG outputs suitable for sensitive health communication.

Conclusion: The TA-RAG framework offers a promising approach for enhancing the suitability of RAG outputs in sensitive peer-support health communication by incorporating tone awareness.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) successfully grounds large language model (LLM) outputs in trusted documents, but factual grounding alone is insufficient for sensitive peer-support health communication. In domains such as HIV peer support, responses must also be accessible, stigma-free, empathetic, and tailored to the recipient. This paper presents TA-RAG, a lightweight, prompt-based tone-aware RAG framework that embeds explicit tone control into a RAG pipeline without requiring model fine-tuning. We operationalise tone across four core components: stigma-free rewriting, readability adjustment, recipient adaptation, and empathy rephrasing. We evaluate TA-RAG through component-level tests using questions derived from HIV Online Learning Australia (HOLA), UNAIDS terminology guidance, readability metrics, peer-support standards from National Association of People with HIV Australia (NAPWHA), and a public empathy dataset. Results show that the TA-RAG's components improve their targeted communication quality while preserving key content. These findings emphasise that prompt-based tone control is a potential direction for making RAG outputs suitable for sensitive peer-support health communication.

</details>


### [110] [Korean Culture into LLM Alignment: Toward Cultural Coherence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06797)
*MinJae Jung,Minwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method to improve cultural coherence in large language models, specifically for Korean culture, by creating a culturally-adapted safe-response policy and fine-tuning models to produce more culturally safe responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The current approach to cultural aspects in large language models focuses on suppressing harmful outputs, but there is a need for a constructive approach that defines what a culturally coherent response is.

Method: The authors design an alignment-data pipeline using a prompt-based LLM seed generator to expand a Korean harm taxonomy, and fine-tune models using DPO on triplets of prompts, candidate responses, and culturally-adapted guidelines.

Result: The fine-tuned models show an improved Korean cultural safe rate across six open-weight LLMs, with no significant degradation on Korean general-capability benchmarks, and produce more culturally informed responses.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of a constructive approach to cultural coherence in large language models, specifically for Korean culture, and highlights the importance of culturally-adapted guidelines and fine-tuning methods.

Abstract: Cultural-aspect work on large language models is dominated by a negative target: which outputs to suppress. We argue that a constructive counterpart is also needed, a working definition of what a culturally coherent response is rather than only what it must avoid, and instantiate it for Korean. We design an alignment-data pipeline around a prompt-based LLM seed generator that expands a Korean harm taxonomy, with a Korean-culturally-adapted safe-response policy at its centre: a per-category guideline grounded in Korean legal frameworks, social norms, and interpretive conventions, against which three frontier models each produce a candidate response. DPO fine-tuning on the resulting triplets improves the Korean cultural safe rate across six open-weight LLMs while causing no large degradation on Korean general-capability benchmarks, and qualitative outputs show fine-tuned models naming Korean statutes and institutional procedures and, where appropriate, supplying constructive Korean-context information alongside refusal.

</details>


### [111] [Quantifying Media Representation Dynamics Across 25 Years of News Reporting on Policing-related Deaths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06812)
*Farhan Samir,Jappun Dhillon,Meghna Ravikumar,Syed Ishtiaque Ahmed,Vered Shwartz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The study analyzes 4,000 Canadian news articles on police-involved deaths over 25 years, finding that state bureaucrats' perspectives are overrepresented compared to civilians, and proposes a computational model, PerspectiveGap, to analyze media narratives on policing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to investigate how media represents police-involved deaths and the perspectives presented in news narratives, with a focus on the imbalance between state and civilian viewpoints.

Method: The study uses a novel computational model, PerspectiveGap, to analyze 4,000 news articles from Canada over a quarter-century, examining the representation of perspectives from state bureaucrats and civilian actors.

Result: The study finds that state bureaucrats' perspectives are represented nearly three times more than civilians' in news articles on police-involved deaths, and that civilian representation has increased in recent years, but still remains limited.

Conclusion: The PerspectiveGap framework offers a scalable approach for analyzing media narratives on policing and accountability, highlighting the need for more balanced representation of civilian perspectives in news reporting on police-involved deaths.

Abstract: We perform the largest known computational analysis of Canadian news narratives about police-involved deaths, spanning 4,000 articles from the last quarter-century. We develop a novel computational model, PerspectiveGap, grounded in prior sociological work on media representation of policing. We find that reporting on police-involved deaths on average features perspectives from state bureaucrats at a rate nearly three times as much as perspectives from other members of the public, including relatives, community members, eyewitnesses, lawyers representing the family, or civil liberties groups. A considerable fraction of articles contain no points of view from civilian actors, though civilian representation has increased in recent years. Qualitatively, we find that state bureaucrats' accounts of these deaths tend to be clinical and procedural, while civilian discourse carries considerably more emotional valence. The PerspectiveGap framework developed here can be contextualized to other jurisdictions, offering a scalable approach for analyzing how media systems construct narratives around policing and accountability.

</details>


### [112] [Progress-SQL: Improving Reinforcement Learning for Text-to-SQL via Progressive Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06825)
*Shihao Zhang,Xiaoman Wang,Yuan Liu,Yunshi Lan,Weining Qian*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A multi-turn reinforcement learning framework called Progress-SQL is proposed to improve Text-to-SQL generation by introducing progressive rewards and an Oracle-guided Diagnostic Tree.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for Text-to-SQL generation using reinforcement learning have limitations, as they optimize one-shot rewards defined over a single SQL state, which provide limited guidance for iterative SQL correction.

Method: The proposed framework, Progress-SQL, uses an Oracle-guided Diagnostic Tree (ODT) to abstract SQL queries into clause-level structural profiles and produce diagnostic feedback for next-turn refinement. It also defines a progressive reward that measures the improvement from the initial SQL to the final SQL.

Result: Experiments on BIRD, Spider, and Spider robustness variants demonstrate that the proposed method consistently improves Text-to-SQL performance across both primary and robustness evaluations.

Conclusion: The proposed Progress-SQL framework shows promise in improving Text-to-SQL generation by introducing a multi-turn reinforcement learning approach with progressive rewards.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has recently shown promise in improving large language models for Text-to-SQL generation, yet existing methods typically optimize one-shot rewards defined over a single SQL state. Such rewards provide limited guidance for iterative SQL correction and are insufficient to capture the improvement of multi-turn SQL refinement. In this paper, we propose Progress-SQL, a multi-turn reinforcement learning framework with progressive rewards for Text-to-SQL. Our approach introduces an Oracle-guided Diagnostic Tree (ODT), which abstracts SQL queries into clause-level structural profiles and produces diagnostic feedback for next-turn refinement. To provide dense and robust reward signals, we combine ODT-based structural alignment with lexical alignment and define a progressive reward that measures the improvement from the initial SQL to the final SQL. We further incorporate a progression latency reward that favors earlier correctness and an execution status reward that encourages recovery from the invalid SQL. Experiments on BIRD, Spider, and Spider robustness variants demonstrate that our method consistently improves Text-to-SQL performance across both primary and robustness evaluations.

</details>


### [113] [The Dark Regulome: Disentangling Predictability from Regulation in Genomic Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06834)
*Chahat Baranwal,Aadtya Baranwal,Lakshya Nitin Tandon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The study explores the 'dark regulome' in high-grade gliomas, using sequence foundation models to identify regulatory elements that shape synaptogenic gene expression in tumor cells. A new diagnostic approach was developed to distinguish between predictability-driven and regulation-driven variance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand which noncoding elements shape synaptogenic gene expression in tumor cells, particularly in high-grade gliomas that integrate into neural circuits through functional synapses with neurons.

Method: The study utilized three architecturally distinct foundation models (Caduceus-Ph, HyenaDNA, Enformer) and introduced a residualization-and-permutation diagnostic to analyze 30,448 dark genome elements at 92 glioma-relevant loci.

Result: A 10kb proximal-regulatory horizon was identified, and a six-feature linear baseline matched Caduceus top-decile membership at AUC = 0.985. The top-100 elements across all three models were 3.3× enriched for matching brain eQTLs.

Conclusion: The study highlights the importance of the 'dark regulome' in regulating gene expression in high-grade gliomas and provides a general methodological tool for ISM-based regulatory studies.

Abstract: High-grade gliomas integrate into neural circuits through functional synapses with neurons, raising the question of which noncoding elements shape synaptogenic gene expression in tumor cells. The regulatory program written across the dark genome, what we call the $\textit{dark regulome}$, is the natural substrate to probe, and sequence foundation models offer a zero-shot route through in-silico mutagenesis (ISM); yet likelihood-based scoring is tautologically coupled to local sequence predictability, leaving the regulatory interpretation underdetermined. Across three architecturally distinct foundation models (Caduceus-Ph, HyenaDNA, Enformer) and 30,448 dark genome elements at 92 glioma-relevant loci, we introduce a residualization-and-permutation diagnostic that separates predictability-driven from regulation-driven RIS variance. A sharp 10kb proximal-regulatory horizon survives every control we apply, but the LM-derived element-class hierarchy does not: a six-feature linear baseline matches Caduceus top-decile membership at AUC $= 0.985$. Cross-architecture decomposition cleanly separates a sequence-predictability layer (the two language models co-rank long well-predicted transposable elements) from a regulatory-output layer (Enformer alone retains residual cCRE-discriminative signal), with literally zero overlap between the two top-100 lists. Conservation, brain cis-eQTL, and STRING-PPI cross-checks then anchor what biology survives: top-100 elements across all three models are $3.3\times$ enriched per model for matching brain eQTLs ($p_\mathrm{emp} < 5\times 10^{-3}$), while a tempting transposable-element regulatory layer and a striking NRXN1+NLGN1 protein-pair convergence both fail proper permutation tests once those tests are constructed. We deliver the diagnostic as a general methodological tool for any ISM-based regulatory study.

</details>


### [114] [Translate-R1: Cost-Aware Translation Tool Use via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06835)
*Pratik Jayarao,Chaitanya Dwivedi,Himanshu Gupta,Neeraj Varshney,Adithya M Devraj,Meet Vadera,Priyanka Nigam,Bing Yin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents a method to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) across languages by learning a policy that decides when to translate input into the model's dominant language, achieving better results and cost efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The performance gap of LLMs across languages is significant, and existing solutions require manual engineering or are ineffective due to LLMs' overconfidence in their abilities.

Method: The authors propose a language- and domain-adaptive introspection method that assesses the model's comprehension and invokes translation only when necessary, using a single policy learned from reward alone.

Result: The gated policy improves reward by +4.6, +23.5, and +17.5 on High, Low, and XLow resource tiers respectively, and preserves full reward at 63% of the cost, with Pareto-optimality across 87% of the cost-sensitivity range.

Conclusion: The proposed method effectively improves LLMs' performance across languages, achieving better results and cost efficiency, and transfers zero-shot to unseen languages.

Abstract: The performance gap across languages in LLMs is well documented, and closing it natively requires pretraining or fine-tuning on corpora that, for most languages, do not exist. Translation offers an alternative: converting an input into the model's dominant language unlocks its full capabilities at once. Applying translation to every input, however, is wasteful for languages the model already handles, while leaving the choice to the model fails in the opposite way, as LLMs are overconfident and skip the tool even when they cannot understand the input. Prior work resolves this with language-specific rules, domain heuristics, language identifiers, or external routers, each requiring manual engineering. We instead learn a single policy that decides when to translate from reward alone, developing language- and domain-adaptive introspection that assesses its own comprehension and invokes translation only when it cannot solve a task natively.
  Using data built by our answer-preserving translation pipeline, we continue RL on the post-trained Qwen3-4B across 22 languages in 3 resource tiers (High, Low, XLow) and 5 domains, and introduce confidence-gated GSPO for cost-sensitive tool use. The gated policy lifts reward over the baseline by +4.6 on High, +23.5 on Low, and +17.5 on XLow. Against an unconstrained policy that almost always translates, it preserves full reward at 63% of the cost and is Pareto-optimal across 87% of the cost-sensitivity range. Additionally, to simulate behavior on a completely unseen language, we create 2 synthetic languages, where our gated policy improves +18.7 over the overconfident baseline that underutilizes the tool even on these incomprehensible inputs. The policy transfers zero-shot to 9 held-out languages, and we analyze how tool use emerges over training, per language and per domain.

</details>


### [115] [Characterize Then Distill: Mechanistic Reasoning in Large Output Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06840)
*Debjyoti Saha Roy,Byron C. Wallace,Javed A. Aslam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how modern reasoning models achieve strong zero-shot performance on multi-label tasks and proposes a mechanistic distillation strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how modern reasoning models achieve strong zero-shot performance on challenging multi-label tasks.

Method: Characterizing reasoning as a two-phase process: 'shortlisting' of candidates followed by fine-grained reasoning, and developing a mechanistic distillation strategy.

Result: The proposed mechanistic distillation strategy consistently outperforms standard distillation across various datasets.

Conclusion: The study provides insights into the mechanisms of modern reasoning models and proposes an effective distillation strategy for multi-label tasks.

Abstract: Modern reasoning models offer surprisingly strong zero-shot performance on challenging multi-label tasks that require selecting a small set of relevant options from hundreds of thousands to millions of candidate labels. We investigate how they achieve this mechanistically. We characterize reasoning as a two-phase process: A broad "shortlisting" of candidates followed by fine-grained reasoning over the resulting set. We provide evidence across a range of datasets that these steps can be isolated and are complementary. Using this characterization, we develop a mechanistic distillation strategy that consistently outperforms standard distillation.

</details>


### [116] [CRAFT: A Unified Counterfactual Reasoning Framework for Tabular Question Answering and Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06842)
*Chenshuo Pan,Yu Zhao,Jie Zhang,Changzai Pan,Zhenhe Wu,Jiayi Liang,Yujie Mao,Shuangyong Song,Yongxiang Li,Zhongjiang He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new framework called CRAFT is proposed for table reasoning tasks, which uses counterfactual reasoning to improve the performance of large language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Table reasoning remains challenging for large language models, particularly in tasks that require multi-step inference over long and structured tables, and existing approaches are limited by single-direction reasoning.

Method: CRAFT, a unified Counterfactual Reasoning Framework, reformulates tabular question answering and fact verification into a general bidirectional verification process, constructing declarative statements and their counterfactual variants, and integrating evidence via a weighted mechanism.

Result: The approach consistently surpasses representative baselines on table reasoning datasets such as WikiTQ and TabFact, achieving especially large improvements on complex question answering, and significantly mitigates performance gaps between different backbone LLMs.

Conclusion: Counterfactual reasoning effectively overcomes the limitations of single-direction inference, guiding LLMs toward more discerning reasoning and establishing a more principled paradigm for structured reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Table reasoning remains challenging for large language models (LLMs), particularly in tasks that require multi-step inference over long and structured tables. Existing approaches predominantly rely on single-direction reasoning, which limits their ability to explore alternative hypotheses across tasks. In this work, we propose CRAFT, a unified Counterfactual Reasoning Framework that reformulates Tabular question answering and fact verification into a general bidirectional verification process. Our method explicitly constructs both declarative statements and their counterfactual variants. Evidence is then extracted from reasoning along both the original and counterfactual paths, and integrated via a weighted mechanism to arrive at the final answer. Experimental results show that our approach consistently surpasses representative baselines on table reasoning datasets such as WikiTQ and TabFact, achieving especially large improvements on complex question answering. Our framework also significantly mitigates performance gaps between different backbone LLMs. This indicates that counterfactual reasoning effectively overcomes the limitations of single-direction inference, guiding LLMs toward more discerning reasoning and establishing a more principled paradigm for structured reasoning tasks. Our code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [117] [Interpreting Brain Responses to Language with Sparse Features from Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06857)
*Michael A. Lepori,Kendrick Kay,Greta Tuckute*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study uses a new encoding framework to analyze the relationship between human language cortex and artificial language models, finding a nontrivial correspondence between brain and language model representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize the features represented by human language cortex and address criticisms of relating biological and artificial representations as 'black boxes'.

Method: The study introduces Augmented Sparse Encoding Models, which uses sparse autoencoder features and surprisal as a predictor, and applies it to a 7T fMRI dataset of participants listening to linguistically diverse sentences.

Result: The study validates the modeling framework, interprets neural responses, and finds that brain responses are best explained by features that capture general information encoded in language model representations.

Conclusion: The study suggests a nontrivial correspondence between brain and language model language representation, and that the fronto-temporal human language network is predicted by a common set of features across its constituent regions.

Abstract: A central goal of cognitive neuroscience is to characterize the features that are represented by human language cortex. Artificial language models (LMs) have emerged as a powerful tool to address this challenge, but studies relating biological and artificial representations are often criticized as relating one black box to another. The present work introduces Augmented Sparse Encoding Models, an encoding framework that replaces dense LM hidden states with hierarchically-organized sparse autoencoder (SAE) features, while explicitly including surprisal as a predictor. Using this approach, we (i) produce interpretations of neural responses and (ii) test whether model-brain alignment reflects primary or idiosyncratic variation in LM representations. Using a high-field 7T fMRI dataset of eight participants listening to 200 linguistically diverse sentences, we first validate our modeling framework by recovering previous interpretations of voxel populations tuned to processing difficulty and meaning abstractness. We then interpret a previously-uncharacterized (but reliable) voxel population and find that it is tuned to people-related content. Next, we show that the fronto-temporal human language network is predicted by a common set of features across its constituent regions, but find that frontal regions are relatively well-explained by surprisal alone, even in the absence of LM-based features. Finally, we show that brain responses during language processing are not merely predictable from an arbitrary set of LM features. Rather, brain responses are best explained by the features that tend to capture the most general information encoded in LM representations, suggesting a nontrivial correspondence between brain and LM language representation.

</details>


### [118] [Are Large Language Models Suitable for Graph Computation? Progress and Prospects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06865)
*Yuting Zhang,Yi Han,Kai Wang,Wei Ni,Angela Bonifati,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper reviews the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for graph computation, categorizing their roles as executors or planners, and discusses their strengths, limitations, and future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation behind this paper is to provide a comprehensive review of LLMs for graph computation, addressing the unclear role of LLMs in graph-solving pipelines and the need for a structured understanding of their capabilities and limitations.

Method: The paper uses a role-based taxonomy to categorize LLMs into two paradigms: LLMs as executors and LLMs as planners. It analyzes existing methods based on this taxonomy, highlighting their strengths and limitations.

Result: The review finds that LLMs are promising for simple, small-scale graph tasks but remain unreliable for large-scale and exactness-demanding tasks. It also summarizes available datasets and suggests future directions.

Conclusion: The conclusion of this paper is that while LLMs show promise in graph computation, particularly for simple and small-scale tasks, they are not yet reliable for complex and large-scale tasks, and further research is needed to improve their performance and applicability.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have been increasingly explored for graph computation, where tasks require reasoning over structured relationships and algorithmic operations. Yet, it remains unclear when LLMs can reliably support such computation and how they should be incorporated into graph-solving pipelines. Existing surveys at the intersection of LLMs and graphs primarily focus on graph learning, text-attributed graphs, or graph-language modeling. To bridge this gap, we provide a comprehensive review of LLMs for graph computation through a role-based taxonomy. Specifically, we identify two major paradigms: i) LLMs as executors, where models directly solve graph tasks from graph descriptions and instructions; and ii) LLMs as planners, where models formulate problems, decompose reasoning steps, and invoke external tools or agents for execution. Based on this taxonomy, we analyze the strengths and limitations of current methods. Our review indicates that LLMs are promising for simple, small-scale tasks, but remain unreliable for large-scale and exactness-demanding tasks. Finally, we summarize available datasets and suggest four future directions.

</details>


### [119] [An Expanded Synthetic Conversation Dataset for Multi-Turn Smishing Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06879)
*Carl Lochstampfor,Ayan Roy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The authors present COVA-X, an expanded dataset of 10,985 conversations for detecting smishing scams, and show that transformer models outperform traditional methods when trained on a larger dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to improve the detection of smishing scams, particularly those targeting elderly individuals, by creating a larger and more accurate dataset.

Method: The authors use a improved generation pipeline to create the COVA-X dataset, and train and evaluate various machine learning models, including transformer models and traditional methods like XGBoost.

Result: The results show that the Longformer transformer model outperforms XGBoost on all evaluation metrics, achieving 79.71% accuracy and 0.7786 macro F1, and that dataset refinement improves the performance of all classifier architectures.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that transformer models require larger conversational corpora to realize their contextual advantages, and that dataset refinement is crucial for improving the accuracy of smishing scam detection models.

Abstract: Our prior work introduced COVA, a synthetically generated multi-turn conversational smishing dataset of 3,201 labeled conversations, establishing baseline detection benchmarks across eight models. While XGBoost with TF-IDF features achieved the best performance, with 72.5\% accuracy and 0.691 macro F1, transformer models underperformed, which was attributed to input truncation and insufficient training data. We present COVA-X, an expanded dataset of 10,985 conversations spanning eight elder-targeted scam categories, produced by an improved generation pipeline addressing contamination, label mismatch, stage-direction bleed, and prompt-design failures from the first iteration. Retraining all classifiers on the expanded dataset yields the central finding of this work: Longformer now surpasses XGBoost on all evaluation metrics, achieving 79.71\% accuracy and 0.7786 macro F1 compared with 78.43\% and 0.7563 for XGBoost. This directly confirms that transformer models require larger conversational corpora to realize their contextual advantages. We additionally document a quality life-cycle including a 12.7$\times$ improvement in label correction rate, from 49.8\% to 3.9\%, an architectural intervention reducing virtual-kidnapping artifact rates from 67.1\% to 46.5\%, and a per-scam-type outcome analysis showing that scam categories modulate results in mechanism-consistent ways. A pre/post-cleanup sensitivity analysis confirms that dataset refinement recovers genuine label-relevant signal across all three classifier architectures.

</details>


### [120] [EASE-TTT: Evidence-Aligned Selective Test-Time Training for Long-Context Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06906)
*Xiaopeng Yuan,Zebin Wang,Suwen Wang,Zongxin Yang,Haohan Wang,Yushun Dong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EASE-TTT, a test-time training framework, improves long-context question answering by aligning evidence chunks with attention supervision, outperforming baselines on six QA tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for long-context question answering struggle to adapt to smaller language models, and current test-time adaptation methods do not effectively localize evidence.

Method: EASE-TTT uses within-context retrieval to identify evidence chunks and converts them into a soft attention supervision target to guide query-side adaptation.

Result: EASE-TTT achieves the strongest macro-average performance on six LongBench QA tasks and three small decoder-only language models compared to baselines.

Conclusion: EASE-TTT supports evidence-aligned test-time adaptation in long-context QA, providing a more effective approach for smaller language models.

Abstract: Long-context question answering (QA) remains challenging for smaller language models even when answer-bearing evidence is already present in the input. Existing within-context retrieval methods localize and expose candidate evidence chunks for the question, but they stop at input-level evidence exposure rather than adapting the query-side attention parameters that control how the model allocates attention over full-context positions.
